Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi, Doctor. Well, to be completely honest with you, my job actually sent me here. Or, well, they strongly suggested I come. I do general maintenance and repair work—mostly commercial buildings, fixing a little bit of everything, drywall, plumbing, whatever breaks down. I’ve been doing it a long time, and I’m really good with my hands, but the administrative stuff... it’s just gotten completely out of hand. 

What really brought me in is that I just keep forgetting things. Important things. Like scheduled callbacks. A tenant will ask me to come back and check a valve once the pressure builds up, and I swear I mean to do it, but it just evaporates from my brain. And the work orders! They give us these tablets now, and I’ll look at a work order, put the tablet down to grab my wrench, and completely forget it exists. I’m missing basic follow-ups I promised people, too. Like, I'll tell someone I need to order a specific part and that I'll call them by Friday. Friday comes and goes, and I don't even remember the conversation until they complain to management. 

It’s all just piled up so much lately. I have this huge backlog of half-finished tickets and angry voicemails. My boss finally pulled me into the office last week. There’s a lead maintenance position opening up, and he told me I’m the best fixer they have, but he basically said work sent me here to get my head straight before I lose my shot at moving up. If I don't get this under control, they're going to pass me over for the promotion, and I really, really need the pay bump, so... yeah. That's why I'm here. I just can't keep dropping the ball like this.

Oh, gosh, no, it’s not just recently. I mean, my boss probably thinks it’s just the new tablet system or the volume of tickets lately, but honestly? I remember being exactly this way way back in grade school. Like, long, long before I ever had to worry about job pressure or promotions or any of that adult stuff. 

I used to drive my teachers absolutely up the wall. They’d always tell my parents—I can still picture my fourth-grade teacher, Mrs. Higgins, at one of those parent-teacher conferences—she’d always say, "She’s such a bright girl, she really is, but..." There was always a "but." And it was always about how I was constantly misplacing my papers. I’d do the homework, I swear I’d sit at the kitchen table and do it, and then by the time I got to school, the paper had just vanished into thin air. My backpack was like a black hole. 

Or they’d give us instructions for a project, and I’d somehow just completely miss parts of the directions. Like, I’d hear the first step, and maybe the last thing they said, but the middle part just didn't register at all. And doing actual work in class? If the teacher didn't come by and tap on my desk to give me a reminder, I’d never finish my classwork. I’d be off taking my pen apart to see how the spring worked or staring at the radiator. I always needed those constant reminders just to get through a basic worksheet. 

So, yeah, it’s definitely not a new thing at all. It’s just... I guess when you’re a kid, adults are there to catch you when you drop the ball, you know? They tap your desk. But now it’s just me, and nobody's tapping my shoulder to remind me to call a tenant back about a leak, so it's just catching up to me so much worse now.
=====================================

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
You know, it’s funny you ask that, because people always assume that since I work in maintenance and I’m on my feet all day, I must have a ton of energy. But honestly? No, not at all. I’ve never really been the restless type. 

I mean, my brother, now he was a holy terror growing up—always bouncing off the walls, couldn't sit still at the dinner table for two seconds without kicking his legs or tipping his chair back. But me? I was never like that. If I had to sit still, I could sit still. I'd just be completely zoned out while I did it. Even now, if I sit down in my recliner after a shift, I am perfectly content just sinking into the cushions. Half the time I sit there so long staring into space that I completely forget to make dinner until my stomach starts growling at nine o'clock at night. It's not a nervous energy where I feel like my motor is running, you know? 

And as for blurting things out or being impulsive... gosh, no. I wouldn't say that either. I'm actually pretty quiet. Sometimes I think I'm too quiet. Like, we have these Monday morning safety meetings at the shop, and the other guys are always cracking jokes, talking over each other, interrupting the supervisor. Not me. I’m usually just sitting there in the back row. I might be completely in my own world, you know, staring at a water stain on the ceiling tiles and trying to figure out if it's from a leaky roof or condensation from the HVAC duct, but I’m definitely not interrupting anybody. 

And making quick decisions? Oh, absolutely not. If anything, I take way, way too long to decide things. I will literally stand in the plumbing aisle at the supply house for twenty minutes just staring at two different brands of pipe thread sealant. I'll read the labels, put them back, pick them up again, trying to figure out which one is better, even though I know perfectly well they do the exact same thing and cost the exact same amount. My boss always tells me I overthink the small stuff. 

So, yeah, no, I don't really get that restless, jumpy, impulsive feeling at all. I've always been more of a... well, just a slow-moving daydreamer, I guess.

Important things. Like scheduled callbacks. A tenant will ask me to come back and check a valve once the pressure builds up, and I swear I mean to do it, but it just evaporates from my brain. And the work orders! They give us these tablets now, and I’ll look at a work order, put the tablet down to grab my wrench, and completely forget it exists. I’m missing basic follow-ups I promised people, too. Like, I'll tell someone I need to order a specific part and that I'll call them by Friday. Friday comes and goes, and I don't even remember the conversation until they complain to management. \n\nIt’s all just piled up so much lately. I have this huge backlog of half-finished tickets and angry voicemails. My boss finally pulled me into the office last week. There’s a lead maintenance position opening up, and he told me I’m the best fixer they have, but he basically said work sent me here to get my head straight before I lose my shot at moving up. If I don't get this under control, they're going to pass me over for the promotion, and I really, really need the pay bump, so... yeah. That's why I'm here. I just can't keep dropping the ball like this.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced this kind of forgetfulness and trouble following through on tasks for as long as you can remember, even back in school, or is this something that's really only become a problem recently?\n\nPatient: Oh, gosh, no, it’s not just recently. I mean, my boss probably thinks it’s just the new tablet system or the volume of tickets lately, but honestly? I remember being exactly this way way back in grade school. Like, long, long before I ever had to worry about job pressure or promotions or any of that adult stuff. \n\nI used to drive my teachers absolutely up the wall. They’d always tell my parents—I can still picture my fourth-grade teacher, Mrs. Higgins, at one of those parent-teacher conferences—she’d always say, \"She’s such a bright girl, she really is, but...\" There was always a \"but.\" And it was always about how I was constantly misplacing my papers. I’d do the homework, I swear I’d sit at the kitchen table and do it, and then by the time I got to school, the paper had just vanished into thin air. My backpack was like a black hole. \n\nOr they’d give us instructions for a project, and I’d somehow just completely miss parts of the directions. Like, I’d hear the first step, and maybe the last thing they said, but the middle part just didn't register at all. And doing actual work in class? If the teacher didn't come by and tap on my desk to give me a reminder, I’d never finish my classwork. I’d be off taking my pen apart to see how the spring worked or staring at the radiator. I always needed those constant reminders just to get through a basic worksheet. \n\nSo, yeah, it’s definitely not a new thing at all. It’s just... I guess when you’re a kid, adults are there to catch you when you drop the ball, you know? They tap your desk. But now it’s just me, and nobody's tapping my shoulder to remind me to call a tenant back about a leak, so it's just catching up to me so much worse now.\n\nDoctor: Growing up, and even now, have you often felt restless, fidgety, or like you had to be constantly moving, or found yourself acting impulsively—like blurting things out or making quick decisions without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: You know, it’s funny you ask that, because people always assume that since I work in maintenance and I’m on my feet all day, I must have a ton of energy. But honestly? No, not at all. I’ve never really been the restless type. \n\nI mean, my brother, now he was a holy terror growing up—always bouncing off the walls, couldn't sit still at the dinner table for two seconds without kicking his legs or tipping his chair back. But me? I was never like that. If I had to sit still, I could sit still. I'd just be completely zoned out while I did it. Even now, if I sit down in my recliner after a shift, I am perfectly content just sinking into the cushions. Half the time I sit there so long staring into space that I completely forget to make dinner until my stomach starts growling at nine o'clock at night. It's not a nervous energy where I feel like my motor is running, you know? \n\nAnd as for blurting things out or being impulsive... gosh, no. I wouldn't say that either. I'm actually pretty quiet. Sometimes I think I'm too quiet. Like, we have these Monday morning safety meetings at the shop, and the other guys are always cracking jokes, talking over each other, interrupting the supervisor. Not me. I’m usually just sitting there in the back row. I might be completely in my own world, you know, staring at a water stain on the ceiling tiles and trying to figure out if it's from a leaky roof or condensation from the HVAC duct, but I’m definitely not interrupting anybody. \n\nAnd making quick decisions? Oh, absolutely not. If anything, I take way, way too long to decide things. I will literally stand in the plumbing aisle at the supply house for twenty minutes just staring at two different brands of pipe thread sealant. I'll read the labels, put them back, pick them up again, trying to figure out which one is better, even though I know perfectly well they do the exact same thing and cost the exact same amount. My boss always tells me I overthink the small stuff. \n\nSo, yeah, no, I don't really get that restless, jumpy, impulsive feeling at all. I've always been more of a... well, just a slow-moving daydreamer, I guess.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you experienced this kind of forgetfulness and trouble following through on tasks for as long as you can remember, even back in school, or is this something that's really only become a problem recently?\n\nPatient: Oh, gosh, no, it’s not just recently. I mean, my boss probably thinks it’s just the new tablet system or the volume of tickets lately, but honestly? I remember being exactly this way way back in grade school. Like, long, long before I ever had to worry about job pressure or promotions or any of that adult stuff. \n\nI used to drive my teachers absolutely up the wall. They’d always tell my parents—I can still picture my fourth-grade teacher, Mrs. Higgins, at one of those parent-teacher conferences—she’d always say, \"She’s such a bright girl, she really is, but...\" There was always a \"but.\" And it was always about how I was constantly misplacing my papers. I’d do the homework, I swear I’d sit at the kitchen table and do it, and then by the time I got to school, the paper had just vanished into thin air. My backpack was like a black hole. \n\nOr they’d give us instructions for a project, and I’d somehow just completely miss parts of the directions. Like, I’d hear the first step, and maybe the last thing they said, but the middle part just didn't register at all. And doing actual work in class? If the teacher didn't come by and tap on my desk to give me a reminder, I’d never finish my classwork. I’d be off taking my pen apart to see how the spring worked or staring at the radiator. I always needed those constant reminders just to get through a basic worksheet. \n\nSo, yeah, it’s definitely not a new thing at all. It’s just... I guess when you’re a kid, adults are there to catch you when you drop the ball, you know? They tap your desk. But now it’s just me, and nobody's tapping my shoulder to remind me to call a tenant back about a leak, so it's just catching up to me so much worse now.\n\nDoctor: Growing up, and even now, have you often felt restless, fidgety, or like you had to be constantly moving, or found yourself acting impulsively—like blurting things out or making quick decisions without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: You know, it’s funny you ask that, because people always assume that since I work in maintenance and I’m on my feet all day, I must have a ton of energy. But honestly? No, not at all. I’ve never really been the restless type. \n\nI mean, my brother, now he was a holy terror growing up—always bouncing off the walls, couldn't sit still at the dinner table for two seconds without kicking his legs or tipping his chair back. But me? I was never like that. If I had to sit still, I could sit still. I'd just be completely zoned out while I did it. Even now, if I sit down in my recliner after a shift, I am perfectly content just sinking into the cushions. Half the time I sit there so long staring into space that I completely forget to make dinner until my stomach starts growling at nine o'clock at night. It's not a nervous energy where I feel like my motor is running, you know? \n\nAnd as for blurting things out or being impulsive... gosh, no. I wouldn't say that either. I'm actually pretty quiet. Sometimes I think I'm too quiet. Like, we have these Monday morning safety meetings at the shop, and the other guys are always cracking jokes, talking over each other, interrupting the supervisor. Not me. I’m usually just sitting there in the back row. I might be completely in my own world, you know, staring at a water stain on the ceiling tiles and trying to figure out if it's from a leaky roof or condensation from the HVAC duct, but I’m definitely not interrupting anybody. \n\nAnd making quick decisions? Oh, absolutely not. If anything, I take way, way too long to decide things. I will literally stand in the plumbing aisle at the supply house for twenty minutes just staring at two different brands of pipe thread sealant. I'll read the labels, put them back, pick them up again, trying to figure out which one is better, even though I know perfectly well they do the exact same thing and cost the exact same amount. My boss always tells me I overthink the small stuff. \n\nSo, yeah, no, I don't really get that restless, jumpy, impulsive feeling at all. I've always been more of a... well, just a slow-moving daydreamer, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you notice this same kind of forgetfulness and difficulty following through—like missing bill payments, losing track of household chores, or forgetting personal appointments—in your day-to-day home life as well?\n\nPatient: Well, you’d think so, right? If my brain is like a sieve at work, you’d think my house would be a complete disaster area. But honestly... no, I really haven't noticed it being a problem at home. \n\nTake the bills, for example. I don't think I've missed a payment in... gosh, maybe a decade? But that\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you say you haven't missed a bill payment in a decade, is that because you've set up things like autopay or reminders to handle it automatically, or do you actively remember and manage these tasks on your own without any special systems in place?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 49 / maintenance_or_repair_worker_general / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Daily_Activities_Forgetfulness\nmanifestation: \"What brought me in is I keep forgetting scheduled callbacks, work orders, and even basic follow-ups I promised people, and now it’s piled up so much that work sent me here before I lose my shot at moving up.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, no, it’s not that I just naturally remember it all, I suppose. I mean, I do use autopay for the big stuff—the mortgage, the electric bill, the water bill. I set that up years ago because, well, why wouldn't you, right? It just makes sense. But even for the things that aren't on autopay, like my credit card or my internet bill, I just... I don't know, I just handle it. I usually sit down on Sunday mornings with my coffee—I have this specific mug, it’s got a chipped handle, but it’s my favorite—and I just go through the mail and pay whatever needs paying right then and there. It’s just a routine. I don't need alarms buzzing at me or anything like that.

But work? Work is a completely different story. That's... that's the whole reason I'm sitting in this chair right now, honestly. It’s the forgetfulness at work that’s completely out of control. I’ll get a call from a tenant in 4B about a leaky faucet, and I’ll say, "Sure thing, Mrs. Gable, I’ll be up there in twenty minutes," and then I’ll hang up, and... poof. It’s gone. Completely gone from my brain. I’ll start walking toward the elevator, and then the property manager will stop me to ask about the snowblower maintenance, and by the time that conversation is over, I have absolutely no memory of Mrs. Gable or her faucet. 

And it’s not just one or two things, it’s everything. I’m forgetting scheduled callbacks, I’m losing track of work orders... I mean, we have this new tablet system where everything is supposed to be logged, but if I don't type it in the exact second I get the call, I forget to do it later. And I'm forgetting basic follow-ups I promised people. Like, I’ll tell a guy I’m going to check if we have a specific air filter in the supply closet, and three days later he’s asking me about it, and I’m just staring at him blankly because I never even went to the closet. 

It’s just piled up so much. The complaints are stacking up. My supervisor finally pulled me into his office last week—he’s usually a pretty laid-back guy, you know, he used to be a plumber himself so he gets how chaotic the job can be—but he sat me down and said, "Look, you do good work when you actually do it, but things are slipping through the cracks." There’s a senior maintenance position opening up next month, and I really, really want it. I need it, honestly. But he basically told me that work sent me here to get this figured out, because if I keep dropping the ball like this, I’m going to lose my shot at moving up. So it’s really just the work stuff that’s falling apart on me, not the home stuff.
=====================================

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Honestly, with hobbies and texts... I don't really have much of a social life to mess up, to be perfectly honest with you. My sister texts me pictures of her dog, and I text back a thumbs up, and that's about it. And as for personal projects, by the time I get home from a shift, I'm usually just too beat to even start anything. 

But when you say "difficulty following through"... I know you were asking about home stuff, but my mind just goes right back to the shop, because that is exactly the phrase my supervisor used last week. Follow-through. It’s like I just can't let it go, because it's driving me crazy. 

It’s just... okay, here is exactly what happens. I’ll get a work order, right? Let's say it's an electrical issue in one of the older units on the third floor. I’ll haul all my gear up there, I’ll begin the maintenance job, I'll troubleshoot the whole thing—which, by the way, in those old buildings is a nightmare because the wiring was done in the seventies and half of it makes absolutely no sense—but I’ll figure it out. I’ll do the actual fix. I'll swap out the faulty breaker, strip the wires, test the circuit, make sure everything is perfectly safe and working better than before. The hard part is done! The problem is solved. 

But then... I don't know what happens in my brain. It's like I mentally check out the exact second the physical problem is solved. I will somehow leave the last, easiest steps completely undone. I'll pack up my tool bag and just walk away without closing the ticket on the tablet, or I'll forget to use my marker and label the panel so the next guy knows what goes where, or I won't even knock on the bedroom door to check back with the tenant to tell them their outlets are working again. 

I just... move on. It drives my boss absolutely up the wall. He’s like, "You did the hard work, you fixed the thing, why didn't you just push the button on the screen to say you fixed it?" And I never have a good answer for him. It’s like once the puzzle is solved, my brain just wipes the rest of the steps completely clean.

I mean, I do use autopay for the big stuff—the mortgage, the electric bill, the water bill. I set that up years ago because, well, why wouldn't you, right? It just makes sense. But even for the things that aren't on autopay, like my credit card or my internet bill, I just... I don't know, I just handle it. I usually sit down on Sunday mornings with my coffee—I have this specific mug, it’s got a chipped handle, but it’s my favorite—and I just go through the mail and pay whatever needs paying right then and there. It’s just a routine. I don't need alarms buzzing at me or anything like that.\n\nBut work? Work is a completely different story. That's... that's the whole reason I'm sitting in this chair right now, honestly. It’s the forgetfulness at work that’s completely out of control. I’ll get a call from a tenant in 4B about a leaky faucet, and I’ll say, \"Sure thing, Mrs. Gable, I’ll be up there in twenty minutes,\" and then I’ll hang up, and... poof. It’s gone. Completely gone from my brain. I’ll start walking toward the elevator, and then the property manager will stop me to ask about the snowblower maintenance, and by the time that conversation is over, I have absolutely no memory of Mrs. Gable or her faucet. \n\nAnd it’s not just one or two things, it’s everything. I’m forgetting scheduled callbacks, I’m losing track of work orders... I mean, we have this new tablet system where everything is supposed to be logged, but if I don't type it in the exact second I get the call, I forget to do it later. Like, I’ll tell a guy I’m going to check if we have a specific air filter in the supply closet, and three days later he’s asking me about it, and I’m just staring at him blankly because I never even went to the closet. \n\nIt’s just piled up so much. So it’s really just the work stuff that’s falling apart on me, not the home stuff.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work and paying bills, do you notice this same kind of forgetfulness or difficulty following through in other areas—like keeping up with hobbies, replying to texts or social plans, or finishing personal projects around the house?\n\nPatient: Honestly, with hobbies and texts... I don't really have much of a social life to mess up, to be perfectly honest with you. My sister texts me pictures of her dog, and I text back a thumbs up, and that's about it. And as for personal projects, by the time I get home from a shift, I'm usually just too beat to even start anything. \n\nBut when you say \"difficulty following through\"... I know you were asking about home stuff, but my mind just goes right back to the shop, because that is exactly the phrase my supervisor used last week. Follow-through. It’s like I just can't let it go, because it's driving me crazy. \n\nIt’s just... okay, here is exactly what happens. I’ll get a work order, right? Let's say it's an electrical issue in one of the older units on the third floor. I’ll haul all my gear up there, I’ll begin the maintenance job, I'll troubleshoot the whole thing—which, by the way, in those old buildings is a nightmare because the wiring was done in the seventies and half of it makes absolutely no sense—but I’ll figure it out. I’ll do the actual fix. I'll swap out the faulty breaker, strip the wires, test the circuit, make sure everything is perfectly safe and working better than before. The hard part is done! The problem is solved. \n\nBut then... I don't know what happens in my brain. It's like I mentally check out the exact second the physical problem is solved. I'll pack up my tool bag and just walk away without closing the ticket on the tablet, or I'll forget to use my marker and label the panel so the next guy knows what goes where, or I won't even knock on the bedroom door to check back with the tenant to tell them their outlets are working again. \n\nI just... move on. It drives my boss absolutely up the wall. He’s like, \"You did the hard work, you fixed the thing, why didn't you just push the button on the screen to say you fixed it?\" And I never have a good answer for him. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, it's a nightmare. It is an absolute nightmare. You asked about home, and sure, maybe I'll set my phone down on the kitchen counter and have to pat down my jacket pockets to find my car keys before I leave the house, but at work? At work, it is a constant, exhausting scavenger hunt. 

I am constantly—and I mean constantly—hunting for that new tablet they gave us, or my ring of master keys, or my flashlight. And it's not even just the big things, it’s the tool I literally just had in my hand a minute ago. I’ll be under a sink, right? I’ll have my favorite wrench—it has this bright yellow grip so I'm supposed to be able to see it easily in the dark—and I’ll use it, set it down on the mat for two seconds to grab a piece of Teflon tape, and when I reach back for the wrench, it’s gone. It’s like it just vanished into thin air. I’ll end up pulling everything out of my tool bag, checking all my pockets, crawling around on the floor... 

It’s gotten bad enough that it actively holds up jobs. I'll be in a unit for twice as long as a basic repair should take just because I'm looking for my own equipment. And the tenants notice, you know? They’re standing there in their kitchens or living rooms, watching me spin in circles looking for a pair of wire strippers, and it just makes me look so incredibly careless. Like I’m some kind of amateur who doesn't respect her own tools. And I do know what I'm doing, I really do! I've been fixing things my whole life. But how can I prove to my supervisor that I'm ready for that senior position when I have to stop a job halfway through because I lost the flashlight I was just holding? It's just humiliating, honestly.

I'm Dr. [Name]. To start, can you tell me what's been bothering you or what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi, Doctor. Well, to be completely honest with you, my job actually sent me here. Or, well, they strongly suggested I come. I do general maintenance and repair work—mostly commercial buildings, fixing a little bit of everything, drywall, plumbing, whatever breaks down. I’ve been doing it a long time, and I’m really good with my hands, but the administrative stuff... it’s just gotten completely out of hand. \n\nWhat really brought me in is that I just keep forgetting things. Important things. Like scheduled callbacks. A tenant will ask me to come back and check a valve once the pressure builds up, and I swear I mean to do it, but it just evaporates from my brain. And the work orders! They give us these tablets now, and I’ll look at a work order, put the tablet down to grab my wrench, and completely forget it exists. I’m missing basic follow-ups I promised people, too. Like, I'll tell someone I need to order a specific part and that I'll call them by Friday. Friday comes and goes, and I don't even remember the conversation until they complain to management. \n\nIt’s all just piled up so much lately. I have this huge backlog of half-finished tickets and angry voicemails. My boss finally pulled me into the office last week. There’s a lead maintenance position opening up, and he told me I’m the best fixer they have, but he basically said work sent me here to get my head straight before I lose my shot at moving up. If I don't get this under control, they're going to pass me over for the promotion, and I really, really need the pay bump, so... yeah. That's why I'm here. I just can't keep dropping the ball like this.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced this kind of forgetfulness and trouble following through on tasks for as long as you can remember, even back in school, or is this something that's really only become a problem recently?\n\nPatient: Oh, gosh, no, it’s not just recently. I mean, my boss probably thinks it’s just the new tablet system or the volume of tickets lately, but honestly? I remember being exactly this way way back in grade school. Like, long, long before I ever had to worry about job pressure or promotions or any of that adult stuff. \n\nI used to drive my teachers absolutely up the wall. They’d always tell my parents—I can still picture my fourth-grade teacher, Mrs. Higgins, at one of those parent-teacher conferences—she’d always say, \"She’s such a bright girl, she really is, but...\" There was always a \"but.\" And it was always about how I was constantly misplacing my papers. I’d do the homework, I swear I’d sit at the kitchen table and do it, and then by the time I got to school, the paper had just vanished into thin air. My backpack was like a black hole. \n\nOr they’d give us instructions for a project, and I’d somehow just completely miss parts of the directions. Like, I’d hear the first step, and maybe the last thing they said, but the middle part just didn't register at all. And doing actual work in class? If the teacher didn't come by and tap on my desk to give me a reminder, I’d never finish my classwork. I’d be off taking my pen apart to see how the spring worked or staring at the radiator. I always needed those constant reminders just to get through a basic worksheet. \n\nSo, yeah, it’s definitely not a new thing at all. It’s just... I guess when you’re a kid, adults are there to catch you when you drop the ball, you know? They tap your desk. But now it’s just me, and nobody's tapping my shoulder to remind me to call a tenant back about a leak, so it's just catching up to me so much worse now.\n\nDoctor: Growing up, and even now, have you often felt restless, fidgety, or like you had to be constantly moving, or found yourself acting impulsively—like blurting things out or making quick decisions without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: You know, it’s funny you ask that, because people always assume that since I work in maintenance and I’m on my feet all day, I must have a ton of energy. But honestly? No, not at all. I’ve never really been the restless type. \n\nI mean, my brother, now he was a holy terror growing up—always bouncing off the walls, couldn't sit still at the dinner table for two seconds without kicking his legs or tipping his chair back. But me? I was never like that. If I had to sit still, I could sit still. I'd just be completely zoned out while I did it. Even now, if I sit down in my recliner after a shift, I am perfectly content just sinking into the cushions. Half the time I sit there so long staring into space that I completely forget to make dinner until my stomach starts growling at nine o'clock at night. It's not a nervous energy where I feel like my motor is running, you know? \n\nAnd as for blurting things out or being impulsive... gosh, no. I wouldn't say that either. I'm actually pretty quiet. Sometimes I think I'm too quiet. Like, we have these Monday morning safety meetings at the shop, and the other guys are always cracking jokes, talking over each other, interrupting the supervisor. Not me. I’m usually just sitting there in the back row. I might be completely in my own world, you know, staring at a water stain on the ceiling tiles and trying to figure out if it's from a leaky roof or condensation from the HVAC duct, but I’m definitely not interrupting anybody. \n\nAnd making quick decisions? Oh, absolutely not. If anything, I take way, way too long to decide things. I will literally stand in the plumbing aisle at the supply house for twenty minutes just staring at two different brands of pipe thread sealant. I'll read the labels, put them back, pick them up again, trying to figure out which one is better, even though I know perfectly well they do the exact same thing and cost the exact same amount. My boss always tells me I overthink the small stuff. \n\nSo, yeah, no, I don't really get that restless, jumpy, impulsive feeling at all. I've always been more of a... well, just a slow-moving daydreamer, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you notice this same kind of forgetfulness and difficulty following through—like missing bill payments, losing track of household chores, or forgetting personal appointments—in your day-to-day home life as well?\n\nPatient: Well, you’d think so, right? It's not a nervous energy where I feel like my motor is running, you know? \n\nAnd as for blurting things out or being impulsive... gosh, no. I wouldn't say that either. I'm actually pretty quiet. Sometimes I think I'm too quiet. Like, we have these Monday morning safety meetings at the shop, and the other guys are always cracking jokes, talking over each other, interrupting the supervisor. Not me. I’m usually just sitting there in the back row. I might be completely in my own world, you know, staring at a water stain on the ceiling tiles and trying to figure out if it's from a leaky roof or condensation from the HVAC duct, but I’m definitely not interrupting anybody. \n\nAnd making quick decisions? Oh, absolutely not. If anything, I take way, way too long to decide things. I will literally stand in the plumbing aisle at the supply house for twenty minutes just staring at two different brands of pipe thread sealant. I'll read the labels, put them back, pick them up again, trying to figure out which one is better, even though I know perfectly well they do the exact same thing and cost the exact same amount. My boss always tells me I overthink the small stuff. \n\nSo, yeah, no, I don't really get that restless, jumpy, impulsive feeling at all. I've always been more of a... well, just a slow-moving daydreamer, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you notice this same kind of forgetfulness and difficulty following through—like missing bill payments, losing track of household chores, or forgetting personal appointments—in your day-to-day home life as well?\n\nPatient: Well, you’d think so, right? If my brain is like a sieve at work, you’d think my house would be a complete disaster area. But honestly... no, I really haven't noticed it being a problem at home. \n\nTake the bills, for example. I don't think I've missed a payment in... gosh, maybe a decade? But that\n\nDoctor: When you say you haven't missed a bill payment in a decade, is that because you've set up things like autopay or reminders to handle it automatically, or do you actively remember and manage these tasks on your own without any special systems in place?\n\nPatient: Well, no, it’s not that I just naturally remember it all, I suppose. I mean, I do use autopay for the big stuff—the mortgage, the electric bill, the water bill. I set that up years ago because, well, why wouldn't you, right? It just makes sense. But even for the things that aren't on autopay, like my credit card or my internet bill, I just... I don't know, I just handle it. I usually sit down on Sunday mornings with my coffee—I have this specific mug, it’s got a chipped handle, but it’s my favorite—and I just go through the mail and pay whatever needs paying right then and there. It’s just a routine. I don't need alarms buzzing at me or anything like that.\n\nBut work? Work is a completely different story. That's... that's the whole reason I'm sitting in this chair right now, honestly. It’s the forgetfulness at work that’s completely out of control. I’ll get a call from a tenant in 4B about a leaky faucet, and I’ll say, \"Sure thing, Mrs. Gable, I’ll be up there in twenty minutes,\" and then I’ll hang up, and... poof. It’s gone. Completely gone from my brain. I’ll start walking toward the elevator, and then the property manager will stop me to ask about the snowblower maintenance, and by the time that conversation is over, I have absolutely no memory of Mrs. Gable or her faucet. \n\nAnd it’s not just one or two things, it’s everything. I’m forgetting scheduled callbacks, I’m losing track of work orders... I mean, we have this new tablet system where everything is supposed to be logged, but if I don't type it in the exact second I get the call, I forget to do it later. And I'm forgetting basic follow-ups I promised people. Like, I’ll tell a guy I’m going to check if we have a specific air filter in the supply closet, and three days later he’s asking me about it, and I’m just staring at him blankly because I never even went to the closet. \n\nIt’s just piled up so much. The complaints are stacking up. My supervisor finally pulled me into his office last week—he’s usually a pretty laid-back guy, you know, he used to be a plumber himself so he gets how chaotic the job can be—but he sat me down and said, \"Look, you do good work when you actually do it, but things are slipping through the cracks.\" There’s a senior maintenance position opening up next month, and I really, really want it. I need it, honestly. But he basically told me that work sent me here to get this figured out, because if I keep dropping the ball like this, I’m going to lose my shot at moving up. So it’s really just the work stuff that’s falling apart on me, not the home stuff.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work and paying bills, do you notice this same kind of forgetfulness or difficulty following through in other areas—like keeping up with hobbies, replying to texts or social plans, or finishing personal projects around the house?\n\nPatient: Honestly, with hobbies and texts... I don't really have much of a social life to mess up, to be perfectly honest with you. My sister texts me pictures of her dog, and I text back a thumbs up, and that's about it. And as for personal projects, by the time I get home from a shift, I'm usually just too beat to even start anything. \n\nBut when you say \"difficulty following through\"... I know you were asking about home stuff, but my mind just goes right back to the shop, because that is exactly the phrase my supervisor used last week. Follow-through. It’s like I just can't let it go, because it's driving me crazy. \n\nIt’s just... okay, here is exactly what happens. I’ll get a work order, right? Let's say it's an electrical issue in one of the older units on the third floor. I’ll haul all my gear up there, I’ll begin the maintenance job, I'll troubleshoot the whole thing—which, by the way, in those old buildings is a nightmare because the wiring was done in the seventies and half of it makes absolutely no sense—but I’ll figure it out. I’ll do the actual fix. I'll swap out the faulty breaker, strip the wires, test the circuit, make sure everything is perfectly safe and working better than before. The hard part is done! The problem is solved. \n\nBut then... I don't know what happens in my brain. It's like I mentally check out the exact second the physical problem is solved. I will somehow leave the last, easiest steps completely undone. I'll pack up my tool bag and just walk away without closing the ticket on the tablet, or I'll forget to use my marker and label the panel so the next guy knows what goes where, or I won't even knock on the bedroom door to check back with the tenant to tell them their outlets are working again. \n\nI just... move on. It drives my boss absolutely up the wall. He’s like, \"You did the hard work, you fixed the thing, why didn't you just push the button on the screen to say you fixed it?\" And I never have a good answer for him. It’s like once the puzzle is solved, my brain just wipes the rest of the steps completely clean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself losing or misplacing everyday items you need, like your keys, tools, phone, or paperwork, both at work and at home?\n\nPatient: Oh, it's a nightmare. It is an absolute nightmare. You asked about home, and sure, maybe I'll set my phone down on the kitchen counter and have to pat down my jacket pockets to find my car keys before I leave the house, but at work? At work, it is a constant, exhausting scavenger hunt. \n\nI am constantly—and I mean constantly—hunting for that new tablet they gave us, or my ring of master keys, or my flashlight. And it's not even just the big things, it’s the tool I literally just had in my hand a minute ago. I’ll be under a sink, right? I’ll have my favorite wrench—it has this bright yellow grip so I'm supposed to be able to see it easily in the dark—and I’ll use it, set it down on the mat for two seconds to grab a piece of Teflon tape, and when I reach back for the wrench, it’s gone. It’s like it just vanished into thin air. I’ll end up pulling everything out of my tool bag, checking all my pockets, crawling around on the floor... \n\nIt’s gotten bad enough that it actively holds up jobs. I'll be in a unit for twice as long as a basic repair should take just because I'm looking for my own equipment. And the tenants notice, you know? They’re standing there in their kitchens or living rooms, watching me spin in circles looking for a pair of wire strippers, and it just makes me look so incredibly careless. Like I’m some kind of amateur who doesn't respect her own tools. And I do know what I'm doing, I really do! I've been fixing things my whole life. But how can I prove to my supervisor that I'm ready for that senior position when I have to stop a job halfway through because I lost the flashlight I was just holding? It's just humiliating, honestly.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Meetings? Well, we really only have our morning huddles for the maintenance crew, and I mostly just stand in the back and nod through those, but reading... yeah. Reading is a massive problem, especially with that new tablet system I was telling you about earlier. It’s supposed to make everything so streamlined, right? Management is always saying, "Everything you need to know is in the notes!" But it's... it's like my brain just refuses to hold onto the words, no matter how hard I try to lock in.

I mean, here's exactly how it goes. I’ll get a ping for a new digital work order. I’ll pull it up, usually I'm standing right there in the hallway or down in the boiler room, and I’m looking at the screen thinking, okay, I’ve got it, let’s see what’s going on. And I start reading the notes the leasing office typed up—and sometimes they write a whole novel in there about what the tenant said, which really doesn't help, they put in all these details about the tenant's day instead of just telling me the sink is clogged—but anyway, I'm reading it. I am actively trying my absolute best to concentrate on the words. 

But halfway through reading those notes, my mind just... it just slips right off. It completely drifts away. It doesn't even take much to derail me. I'll hear a noise down the hall, like an elevator door opening or a pipe clanking, or I'll just suddenly start thinking about the next job I have to do in unit 7A and whether I have the right drill bit for it. And my eyes are still moving down the screen, you know? Like I'm still physically looking at the tablet. But then I get to the bottom of the screen and I realize... I don't know what I just looked at. I haven't absorbed a single word of it. 

I literally have to go back to the top and read the exact same sentence three, four times just to figure out if I'm supposed to be fixing a garbage disposal or a thermostat. And if a tenant walks by and says hello while I'm trying to read it? Forget it. I have to start the whole process over from scratch. It’s exhausting, having to read the same three sentences over and over just to get my brain to actually register what they mean.

Oh, absolutely not. If anything, I take way, way too long to decide things. I will literally stand in the plumbing aisle at the supply house for twenty minutes just staring at two different brands of pipe thread sealant. I'll read the labels, put them back, pick them up again, trying to figure out which one is better, even though I know perfectly well they do the exact same thing and cost the exact same amount. My boss always tells me I overthink the small stuff. \n\nSo, yeah, no, I don't really get that restless, jumpy, impulsive feeling at all. I’ll do the actual fix. I'll swap out the faulty breaker, strip the wires, test the circuit, make sure everything is perfectly safe and working better than before. The hard part is done! The problem is solved. \n\nBut then... I don't know what happens in my brain. It's like I mentally check out the exact second the physical problem is solved. I’ll have my favorite wrench—it has this bright yellow grip so I'm supposed to be able to see it easily in the dark—and I’ll use it, set it down on the mat for two seconds to grab a piece of Teflon tape, and when I reach back for the wrench, it’s gone. It’s like it just vanished into thin air. I’ll end up pulling everything out of my tool bag, checking all my pockets, crawling around on the floor... \n\nIt’s gotten bad enough that it actively holds up jobs. I’ll start walking toward the elevator, and then the property manager will stop me to ask about the snowblower maintenance, and by the time that conversation is over, I have absolutely no memory of Mrs. Gable or her faucet. \n\nAnd it’s not just one or two things, it’s everything. I’m forgetting scheduled callbacks, I’m losing track of work orders... I mean, we have this new tablet system where everything is supposed to be logged, but if I don't type it in the exact second I get the call, I forget to do it later. And I'm forgetting basic follow-ups I promised people. Like, I’ll tell a guy I’m going to check if we have a specific air filter in the supply closet, and three days later he’s asking me about it, and I’m just staring at him blankly because I never even went to the closet. \n\nIt’s just piled up so much. The complaints are stacking up. My supervisor finally pulled me into his office last week—he’s usually a pretty laid-back guy, you know, he used to be a plumber himself so he gets how chaotic the job can be—but he sat me down and said, \"Look, you do good work when you actually do it, but things are slipping through the cracks.\" There’s a senior maintenance position opening up next month, and I really, really want it. I need it, honestly. But he basically told me that work sent me here to get this figured out, because if I keep dropping the ball like this, I’m going to lose my shot at moving up. So it’s really just the work stuff that’s falling apart on me, not the home stuff.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work and paying bills, do you notice this same kind of forgetfulness or difficulty following through in other areas—like keeping up with hobbies, replying to texts or social plans, or finishing personal projects around the house?\n\nPatient: Honestly, with hobbies and texts... I don't really have much of a social life to mess up, to be perfectly honest with you. My sister texts me pictures of her dog, and I text back a thumbs up, and that's about it. And as for personal projects, by the time I get home from a shift, I'm usually just too beat to even start anything. \n\nBut when you say \"difficulty following through\"... I know you were asking about home stuff, but my mind just goes right back to the shop, because that is exactly the phrase my supervisor used last week. Follow-through. It’s like I just can't let it go, because it's driving me crazy. \n\nIt’s just... okay, here is exactly what happens. I’ll get a work order, right? Let's say it's an electrical issue in one of the older units on the third floor. I’ll haul all my gear up there, I’ll begin the maintenance job, I'll troubleshoot the whole thing—which, by the way, in those old buildings is a nightmare because the wiring was done in the seventies and half of it makes absolutely no sense—but I’ll figure it out. I’ll do the actual fix. I'll swap out the faulty breaker, strip the wires, test the circuit, make sure everything is perfectly safe and working better than before. The hard part is done! The problem is solved. \n\nBut then... I don't know what happens in my brain. It's like I mentally check out the exact second the physical problem is solved. I will somehow leave the last, easiest steps completely undone. I'll pack up my tool bag and just walk away without closing the ticket on the tablet, or I'll forget to use my marker and label the panel so the next guy knows what goes where, or I won't even knock on the bedroom door to check back with the tenant to tell them their outlets are working again. \n\nI just... move on. It drives my boss absolutely up the wall. He’s like, \"You did the hard work, you fixed the thing, why didn't you just push the button on the screen to say you fixed it?\" And I never have a good answer for him. It’s like once the puzzle is solved, my brain just wipes the rest of the steps completely clean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself losing or misplacing everyday items you need, like your keys, tools, phone, or paperwork, both at work and at home?\n\nPatient: Oh, it's a nightmare. It is an absolute nightmare. You asked about home, and sure, maybe I'll set my phone down on the kitchen counter and have to pat down my jacket pockets to find my car keys before I leave the house, but at work? At work, it is a constant, exhausting scavenger hunt. \n\nI am constantly—and I mean constantly—hunting for that new tablet they gave us, or my ring of master keys, or my flashlight. And it's not even just the big things, it’s the tool I literally just had in my hand a minute ago. I’ll be under a sink, right? I’ll have my favorite wrench—it has this bright yellow grip so I'm supposed to be able to see it easily in the dark—and I’ll use it, set it down on the mat for two seconds to grab a piece of Teflon tape, and when I reach back for the wrench, it’s gone. It’s like it just vanished into thin air. I’ll end up pulling everything out of my tool bag, checking all my pockets, crawling around on the floor... \n\nIt’s gotten bad enough that it actively holds up jobs. I'll be in a unit for twice as long as a basic repair should take just because I'm looking for my own equipment. And the tenants notice, you know? They’re standing there in their kitchens or living rooms, watching me spin in circles looking for a pair of wire strippers, and it just makes me look so incredibly careless. Like I’m some kind of amateur who doesn't respect her own tools. And I do know what I'm doing, I really do! I've been fixing things my whole life. But how can I prove to my supervisor that I'm ready for that senior position when I have to stop a job halfway through because I lost the flashlight I was just holding? It's just humiliating, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to stay focused during things like reading a book, sitting through a long conversation, or paying attention in meetings—even when you're trying your best to concentrate?\n\nPatient: Meetings? Well, we really only have our morning huddles for the maintenance crew, and I mostly just stand in the back and nod through those, but reading... yeah. Reading is a massive problem, especially with that new tablet system I was telling you about earlier. It’s supposed to make everything so streamlined, right? Management is always saying, \"Everything you need to know is in the notes!\" But it's... it's like my brain just refuses to hold onto the words, no matter how hard I try to lock in.\n\nI mean, here's exactly how it goes. I’ll get a ping for a new digital work order. I’ll pull it up, usually I'm standing right there in the hallway or down in the boiler room, and I’m looking at the screen thinking, okay, I’ve got it, let’s see what’s going on. And I start reading the notes the leasing office typed up—and sometimes they write a whole novel in there about what the tenant said, which really doesn't help, they put in all these details about the tenant's day instead of just telling me the sink is clogged—but anyway, I'm reading it. I am actively trying my absolute best to concentrate on the words. \n\nBut halfway through reading those notes, my mind just... it just slips right off. It completely drifts away. It doesn't even take much to derail me. I'll hear a noise down the hall, like an elevator door opening or a pipe clanking, or I'll just suddenly start thinking about the next job I have to do in unit 7A and whether I have the right drill bit for it. And my eyes are still moving down the screen, you know? Like I'm still physically looking at the tablet. But then I get to the bottom of the screen and I realize... I don't know what I just looked at. I haven't absorbed a single word of it. \n\nI literally have to go back to the top and read the exact same sentence three, four times just to figure out if I'm supposed to be fixing a garbage disposal or a thermostat. And if a tenant walks by and says hello while I'm trying to read it? Forget it. I have to start the whole process over from scratch. It’s exhausting, having to read the same three sentences over and over just to get my brain to actually register what they mean.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, sad? No, I wouldn't say sad. I mean, I get frustrated, obviously—like I was just telling you with the whole flashlight situation and my supervisor getting on my case—but I'm not, you know, crying in my truck or feeling down about life in general. I actually really like my job! I like working with my hands and figuring out how things tick. It's just the digital tablets and the details that drive me nuts. So, no, not persistently sad or depressed or anything like that. And stressed... well, sure, I get stressed when a tenant is standing there with their arms crossed while I'm tearing apart my tool bag looking for a simple wire nut, but it's just in the moment, you know? It passes as soon as I get out of there. 

And as for my sleep and appetite, those are perfectly fine. I mean, I'm pretty beat at the end of the day, but I'm hauling heavy gear up and down three flights of stairs in older buildings where the elevators are down half the week, so of course I'm tired! But I sleep like a log the second my head hits the pillow. And my appetite hasn't changed a bit. I pack the exact same ham and swiss sandwich and a big thermos of black coffee every single morning, eat it in the truck around noon, never miss a meal.

Because these problems with focusing and losing my tools and checking out early? They didn't just start lately. It's not like I woke up a few months ago and suddenly couldn't read a work order. So, yeah, no new sadness or sleep issues or anything, and definitely no sudden changes, because the concentration stuff has just always, always been there.

And as for personal projects, by the time I get home from a shift, I'm usually just too beat to even start anything. \n\nBut when you say \"difficulty following through\"... I know you were asking about home stuff, but my mind just goes right back to the shop, because that is exactly the phrase my supervisor used last week. Follow-through. It’s like I just can't let it go, because it's driving me crazy. \n\nIt’s just... okay, here is exactly what happens. I’ll get a work order, right? Let's say it's an electrical issue in one of the older units on the third floor. I’ll haul all my gear up there, I’ll begin the maintenance job, I'll troubleshoot the whole thing—which, by the way, in those old buildings is a nightmare because the wiring was done in the seventies and half of it makes absolutely no sense—but I’ll figure it out. I’ll do the actual fix. I'll swap out the faulty breaker, strip the wires, test the circuit, make sure everything is perfectly safe and working better than before. The hard part is done! The problem is solved. \n\nBut then... I don't know what happens in my brain. It's like I mentally check out the exact second the physical problem is solved. I will somehow leave the last, easiest steps completely undone. I'll pack up my tool bag and just walk away without closing the ticket on the tablet, or I'll forget to use my marker and label the panel so the next guy knows what goes where, or I won't even knock on the bedroom door to check back with the tenant to tell them their outlets are working again. \n\nI just... move on. It drives my boss absolutely up the wall. He’s like, \"You did the hard work, you fixed the thing, why didn't you just push the button on the screen to say you fixed it?\" And I never have a good answer for him. It’s like once the puzzle is solved, my brain just wipes the rest of the steps completely clean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself losing or misplacing everyday items you need, like your keys, tools, phone, or paperwork, both at work and at home?\n\nPatient: Oh, it's a nightmare. It is an absolute nightmare. You asked about home, and sure, maybe I'll set my phone down on the kitchen counter and have to pat down my jacket pockets to find my car keys before I leave the house, but at work? At work, it is a constant, exhausting scavenger hunt. \n\nI am constantly—and I mean constantly—hunting for that new tablet they gave us, or my ring of master keys, or my flashlight. And it's not even just the big things, it’s the tool I literally just had in my hand a minute ago. I’ll be under a sink, right? I’ll have my favorite wrench—it has this bright yellow grip so I'm supposed to be able to see it easily in the dark—and I’ll use it, set it down on the mat for two seconds to grab a piece of Teflon tape, and when I reach back for the wrench, it’s gone. It’s like it just vanished into thin air. I’ll end up pulling everything out of my tool bag, checking all my pockets, crawling around on the floor... \n\nIt’s gotten bad enough that it actively holds up jobs. I'll be in a unit for twice as long as a basic repair should take just because I'm looking for my own equipment. And the tenants notice, you know? They’re standing there in their kitchens or living rooms, watching me spin in circles looking for a pair of wire strippers, and it just makes me look so incredibly careless. Like I’m some kind of amateur who doesn't respect her own tools. And I do know what I'm doing, I really do! I've been fixing things my whole life. But how can I prove to my supervisor that I'm ready for that senior position when I have to stop a job halfway through because I lost the flashlight I was just holding? It's just humiliating, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to stay focused during things like reading a book, sitting through a long conversation, or paying attention in meetings—even when you're trying your best to concentrate?\n\nPatient: Meetings? Well, we really only have our morning huddles for the maintenance crew, and I mostly just stand in the back and nod through those, but reading... yeah. Reading is a massive problem, especially with that new tablet system I was telling you about earlier. It’s supposed to make everything so streamlined, right? Management is always saying, \"Everything you need to know is in the notes!\" But it's... it's like my brain just refuses to hold onto the words, no matter how hard I try to lock in.\n\nI mean, here's exactly how it goes. I’ll get a ping for a new digital work order. I’ll pull it up, usually I'm standing right there in the hallway or down in the boiler room, and I’m looking at the screen thinking, okay, I’ve got it, let’s see what’s going on. And I start reading the notes the leasing office typed up—and sometimes they write a whole novel in there about what the tenant said, which really doesn't help, they put in all these details about the tenant's day instead of just telling me the sink is clogged—but anyway, I'm reading it. I am actively trying my absolute best to concentrate on the words. \n\nBut halfway through reading those notes, my mind just... it just slips right off. It completely drifts away. It doesn't even take much to derail me. I'll hear a noise down the hall, like an elevator door opening or a pipe clanking, or I'll just suddenly start thinking about the next job I have to do in unit 7A and whether I have the right drill bit for it. And my eyes are still moving down the screen, you know? Like I'm still physically looking at the tablet. But then I get to the bottom of the screen and I realize... I don't know what I just looked at. I haven't absorbed a single word of it. \n\nI literally have to go back to the top and read the exact same sentence three, four times just to figure out if I'm supposed to be fixing a garbage disposal or a thermostat. And if a tenant walks by and says hello while I'm trying to read it? Forget it. I have to start the whole process over from scratch. It’s exhausting, having to read the same three sentences over and over just to get my brain to actually register what they mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling persistently sad, anxious, or unusually stressed lately, or noticed changes in your sleep, appetite, or energy that started around the same time as these concentration problems?\n\nPatient: Well, sad? No, I wouldn't say sad. I mean, I get frustrated, obviously—like I was just telling you with the whole flashlight situation and my supervisor getting on my case—but I'm not, you know, crying in my truck or feeling down about life in general. I actually really like my job! I like working with my hands and figuring out how things tick. It's just the digital tablets and the details that drive me nuts. So, no, not persistently sad or depressed or anything like that. And stressed... well, sure, I get stressed when a tenant is standing there with their arms crossed while I'm tearing apart my tool bag looking for a simple wire nut, but it's just in the moment, you know? It passes as soon as I get out of there. \n\nAnd as for my sleep and appetite, those are perfectly fine. I mean, I'm pretty beat at the end of the day, but I'm hauling heavy gear up and down three flights of stairs in older buildings where the elevators are down half the week, so of course I'm tired! But I sleep like a log the second my head hits the pillow. And my appetite hasn't changed a bit. I pack the exact same ham and swiss sandwich and a big thermos of black coffee every single morning, eat it in the truck around noon, never miss a meal. \n\nBut the main thing you just said—you asked if any of that started around the same time as these concentration problems. And... well, that's the thing. There isn't really a \"same time.\" Because these problems with focusing and losing my tools and checking out early? They didn't just start lately. It's not like I woke up a few months ago and suddenly couldn't read a work order. It’s been this way... gosh, pretty much my whole life. I remember being exactly like this back in high school, just completely unable to hold onto a thought if I was reading a history textbook, or leaving my locker wide open in the hallway because I finished grabbing my jacket and my brain just completely moved on to the next thing. So, yeah, no new sadness or sleep issues or anything, and definitely no sudden changes, because the concentration stuff has just always, always been there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I’ve been doing it a long time, and I’m really good with my hands, but the administrative stuff... it’s just gotten completely out of hand. \n\nWhat really brought me in is that I just keep forgetting things. Important things. Like scheduled callbacks. A tenant will ask me to come back and check a valve once the pressure builds up, and I swear I mean to do it, but it just evaporates from my brain. And the work orders! They give us these tablets now, and I’ll look at a work order, put the tablet down to grab my wrench, and completely forget it exists. I’m missing basic follow-ups I promised people, too. Like, I'll tell someone I need to order a specific part and that I'll call them by Friday. Friday comes and goes, and I don't even remember the conversation until they complain to management. \n\nIt’s all just piled up so much lately. I have this huge backlog of half-finished tickets and angry voicemails. My boss finally pulled me into the office last week. There’s a lead maintenance position opening up, and he told me I’m the best fixer they have, but he basically said work sent me here to get my head straight before I lose my shot at moving up. If I don't get this under control, they're going to pass me over for the promotion, and I really, really need the pay bump, so... yeah. That's why I'm here. I just can't keep dropping the ball like this.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced this kind of forgetfulness and trouble following through on tasks for as long as you can remember, even back in school, or is this something that's really only become a problem recently?\n\nPatient: Oh, gosh, no, it’s not just recently. I mean, my boss probably thinks it’s just the new tablet system or the volume of tickets lately, but honestly? I remember being exactly this way way back in grade school. Like, long, long before I ever had to worry about job pressure or promotions or any of that adult stuff. \n\nI used to drive my teachers absolutely up the wall. They’d always tell my parents—I can still picture my fourth-grade teacher, Mrs. Higgins, at one of those parent-teacher conferences—she’d always say, \"She’s such a bright girl, she really is, but...\" There was always a \"but.\" And it was always about how I was constantly misplacing my papers. I’d do the homework, I swear I’d sit at the kitchen table and do it, and then by the time I got to school, the paper had just vanished into thin air. I guess when you’re a kid, adults are there to catch you when you drop the ball, you know? They tap your desk. But now it’s just me, and nobody's tapping my shoulder to remind me to call a tenant back about a leak, so it's just catching up to me so much worse now.\n\nDoctor: Growing up, and even now, have you often felt restless, fidgety, or like you had to be constantly moving, or found yourself acting impulsively—like blurting things out or making quick decisions without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: You know, it’s funny you ask that, because people always assume that since I work in maintenance and I’m on my feet all day, I must have a ton of energy. I’ve never really been the restless type. \n\nI mean, my brother, now he was a holy terror growing up—always bouncing off the walls, couldn't sit still at the dinner table for two seconds without kicking his legs or tipping his chair back. Even now, if I sit down in my recliner after a shift, I am perfectly content just sinking into the cushions. Half the time I sit there so long staring into space that I completely forget to make dinner until my stomach starts growling at nine o'clock at night. It's not a nervous energy where I feel like my motor is running, you know? \n\nAnd as for blurting things out or being impulsive... gosh, no. I wouldn't say that either. I'm actually pretty quiet. Sometimes I think I'm too quiet. Like, we have these Monday morning safety meetings at the shop, and the other guys are always cracking jokes, talking over each other, interrupting the supervisor. Not me. I’m usually just sitting there in the back row. Oh, absolutely not. If anything, I take way, way too long to decide things. I will literally stand in the plumbing aisle at the supply house for twenty minutes just staring at two different brands of pipe thread sealant. I'll read the labels, put them back, pick them up again, trying to figure out which one is better, even though I know perfectly well they do the exact same thing and cost the exact same amount. My boss always tells me I overthink the small stuff. \n\nSo, yeah, no, I don't really get that restless, jumpy, impulsive feeling at all. I've always been more of a... well, just a slow-moving daydreamer, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you notice this same kind of forgetfulness and difficulty following through—like missing bill payments, losing track of household chores, or forgetting personal appointments—in your day-to-day home life as well?\n\nPatient: Well, you’d think so, right? If my brain is like a sieve at work, you’d think my house would be a complete disaster area. But honestly... no, I really haven't noticed it being a problem at home. \n\nTake the bills, for example. I mean, I do use autopay for the big stuff—the mortgage, the electric bill, the water bill. I set that up years ago because, well, why wouldn't you, right? It just makes sense. But even for the things that aren't on autopay, like my credit card or my internet bill, I just... I don't know, I just handle it. I usually sit down on Sunday mornings with my coffee—I have this specific mug, it’s got a chipped handle, but it’s my favorite—and I just go through the mail and pay whatever needs paying right then and there. It’s just a routine. I don't need alarms buzzing at me or anything like that.\n\nBut work? Work is a completely different story. That's... that's the whole reason I'm sitting in this chair right now, honestly. It’s the forgetfulness at work that’s completely out of control. I’ll get a call from a tenant in 4B about a leaky faucet, and I’ll say, \"Sure thing, Mrs. Gable, I’ll be up there in twenty minutes,\" and then I’ll hang up, and... poof. It’s gone.
