I’m forgetting scheduled callbacks, I’m losing track of work orders... I mean, we have this new tablet system where everything is supposed to be logged, but if I don't type it in the exact second I get the call, I forget to do it later. And I'm forgetting basic follow-ups I promised people. Like, I’ll tell a guy I’m going to check if we have a specific air filter in the supply closet, and three days later he’s asking me about it, and I’m just staring at him blankly because I never even went to the closet. \n\nIt’s just piled up so much. The complaints are stacking up. My supervisor finally pulled me into his office last week—he’s usually a pretty laid-back guy, you know, he used to be a plumber himself so he gets how chaotic the job can be—but he sat me down and said, \"Look, you do good work when you actually do it, but things are slipping through the cracks.\" There’s a senior maintenance position opening up next month, and I really, really want it. I need it, honestly. But he basically told me that work sent me here to get this figured out, because if I keep dropping the ball like this, I’m going to lose my shot at moving up. So it’s really just the work stuff that’s falling apart on me, not the home stuff.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work and paying bills, do you notice this same kind of forgetfulness or difficulty following through in other areas—like keeping up with hobbies, replying to texts or social plans, or finishing personal projects around the house?\n\nPatient: Honestly, with hobbies and texts... I don't really have much of a social life to mess up, to be perfectly honest with you. My sister texts me pictures of her dog, and I text back a thumbs up, and that's about it. And as for personal projects, by the time I get home from a shift, I'm usually just too beat to even start anything. \n\nBut when you say \"difficulty following through\"... I know you were asking about home stuff, but my mind just goes right back to the shop, because that is exactly the phrase my supervisor used last week. Follow-through. It’s like I just can't let it go, because it's driving me crazy. \n\nIt’s just... okay, here is exactly what happens. I’ll get a work order, right? Let's say it's an electrical issue in one of the older units on the third floor. I’ll haul all my gear up there, I’ll begin the maintenance job, I'll troubleshoot the whole thing—which, by the way, in those old buildings is a nightmare because the wiring was done in the seventies and half of it makes absolutely no sense—but I’ll figure it out. I’ll do the actual fix. I'll swap out the faulty breaker, strip the wires, test the circuit, make sure everything is perfectly safe and working better than before. The hard part is done! The problem is solved. \n\nBut then... I don't know what happens in my brain. It's like I mentally check out the exact second the physical problem is solved. I will somehow leave the last, easiest steps completely undone. I'll pack up my tool bag and just walk away without closing the ticket on the tablet, or I'll forget to use my marker and label the panel so the next guy knows what goes where, or I won't even knock on the bedroom door to check back with the tenant to tell them their outlets are working again. \n\nI just... move on. It drives my boss absolutely up the wall. He’s like, \"You did the hard work, you fixed the thing, why didn't you just push the button on the screen to say you fixed it?\" And I never have a good answer for him. It’s like once the puzzle is solved, my brain just wipes the rest of the steps completely clean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself losing or misplacing everyday items you need, like your keys, tools, phone, or paperwork, both at work and at home?\n\nPatient: Oh, it's a nightmare. It is an absolute nightmare. You asked about home, and sure, maybe I'll set my phone down on the kitchen counter and have to pat down my jacket pockets to find my car keys before I leave the house, but at work? At work, it is a constant, exhausting scavenger hunt. \n\nI am constantly—and I mean constantly—hunting for that new tablet they gave us, or my ring of master keys, or my flashlight. And it's not even just the big things, it’s the tool I literally just had in my hand a minute ago. I’ll be under a sink, right? I’ll have my favorite wrench—it has this bright yellow grip so I'm supposed to be able to see it easily in the dark—and I’ll use it, set it down on the mat for two seconds to grab a piece of Teflon tape, and when I reach back for the wrench, it’s gone. It’s like it just vanished into thin air. I’ll end up pulling everything out of my tool bag, checking all my pockets, crawling around on the floor... \n\nIt’s gotten bad enough that it actively holds up jobs. I'll be in a unit for twice as long as a basic repair should take just because I'm looking for my own equipment. And the tenants notice, you know? They’re standing there in their kitchens or living rooms, watching me spin in circles looking for a pair of wire strippers, and it just makes me look so incredibly careless. Like I’m some kind of amateur who doesn't respect her own tools. And I do know what I'm doing, I really do! I've been fixing things my whole life. But how can I prove to my supervisor that I'm ready for that senior position when I have to stop a job halfway through because I lost the flashlight I was just holding? It's just humiliating, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to stay focused during things like reading a book, sitting through a long conversation, or paying attention in meetings—even when you're trying your best to concentrate?\n\nPatient: Meetings? Well, we really only have our morning huddles for the maintenance crew, and I mostly just stand in the back and nod through those, but reading... yeah. Reading is a massive problem, especially with that new tablet system I was telling you about earlier. It’s supposed to make everything so streamlined, right? Management is always saying, \"Everything you need to know is in the notes!\" But it's... it's like my brain just refuses to hold onto the words, no matter how hard I try to lock in.\n\nI mean, here's exactly how it goes. I’ll get a ping for a new digital work order. I’ll pull it up, usually I'm standing right there in the hallway or down in the boiler room, and I’m looking at the screen thinking, okay, I’ve got it, let’s see what’s going on. And I start reading the notes the leasing office typed up—and sometimes they write a whole novel in there about what the tenant said, which really doesn't help, they put in all these details about the tenant's day instead of just telling me the sink is clogged—but anyway, I'm reading it. I am actively trying my absolute best to concentrate on the words. \n\nBut halfway through reading those notes, my mind just... it just slips right off. It completely drifts away. It doesn't even take much to derail me. I'll hear a noise down the hall, like an elevator door opening or a pipe clanking, or I'll just suddenly start thinking about the next job I have to do in unit 7A and whether I have the right drill bit for it. And my eyes are still moving down the screen, you know? Like I'm still physically looking at the tablet. But then I get to the bottom of the screen and I realize... I don't know what I just looked at. I haven't absorbed a single word of it. \n\nI literally have to go back to the top and read the exact same sentence three, four times just to figure out if I'm supposed to be fixing a garbage disposal or a thermostat. And if a tenant walks by and says hello while I'm trying to read it? Forget it. I have to start the whole process over from scratch. It’s exhausting, having to read the same three sentences over and over just to get my brain to actually register what they mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling persistently sad, anxious, or unusually stressed lately, or noticed changes in your sleep, appetite, or energy that started around the same time as these concentration problems?\n\nPatient: Well, sad? No, I wouldn't say sad. I mean, I get frustrated, obviously—like I was just telling you with the whole flashlight situation and my supervisor getting on my case—but I'm not, you know, crying in my truck or feeling down about life in general. I actually really like my job! I like working with my hands and figuring out how things tick. It's just the digital tablets and the details that drive me nuts. So, no, not persistently sad or depressed or anything like that. And stressed... well, sure, I get stressed when a tenant is standing there with their arms crossed while I'm tearing apart my tool bag looking for a simple wire nut, but it's just in the moment, you know? It passes as soon as I get out of there. \n\nAnd as for my sleep and appetite, those are perfectly fine. I mean, I'm pretty beat at the end of the day, but I'm hauling heavy gear up and down three flights of stairs in older buildings where the elevators are down half the week, so of course I'm tired! But I sleep like a log the second my head hits the pillow. And my appetite hasn't changed a bit. It's not like I woke up a few months ago and suddenly couldn't read a work order. It’s been this way... gosh, pretty much my whole life. I remember being exactly like this back in high school, just completely unable to hold onto a thought if I was reading a history textbook, or leaving my locker wide open in the hallway because I finished grabbing my jacket and my brain just completely moved on to the next thing. So, yeah, no new sadness or sleep issues or anything, and definitely no sudden changes, because the concentration stuff has just always, always been there.\n\nDoctor: Do you use alcohol, recreational drugs, or a lot of caffeine, or have you ever been told you have a thyroid problem or sleep condition like sleep apnea?\n\nPatient: Alcohol or drugs? Oh, definitely not. I mean, I might have a single beer if I'm at my brother's house for a summer barbecue or something, but that is absolutely it. Recreational drugs, never. In my line of work, you really can't be messing around with anything like that anyway. I mean, I'm dealing with heavy power tools, high-voltage electrical wiring, main plumbing lines where if you mess up, you could flood an entire apartment building. You make one careless mistake with a 220-volt line because your head isn't clear, and that's a really bad day, you know? Plus, the management company does random screenings for the maintenance staff, and I have been at this job way too long to risk my livelihood over something foolish. \n\nNow, caffeine... you asked about a lot of caffeine. It’s like I just can't let it go, because it's driving me crazy. \n\nIt’s just... okay, here is exactly what happens. I’ll get a work order, right? Let's say it's an electrical issue in one of the older units on the third floor. I’ll haul all my gear up there, I’ll begin the maintenance job, I'll troubleshoot the whole thing—which, by the way, in those old buildings is a nightmare because the wiring was done in the seventies and half of it makes absolutely no sense—but I’ll figure it out. I’ll do the actual fix. I'll swap out the faulty breaker, strip the wires, test the circuit, make sure everything is perfectly safe and working better than before. The hard part is done! The problem is solved. \n\nBut then... I don't know what happens in my brain. It's like I mentally check out the exact second the physical problem is solved. I will somehow leave the last, easiest steps completely undone. I'll pack up my tool bag and just walk away without closing the ticket on the tablet, or I'll forget to use my marker and label the panel so the next guy knows what goes where, or I won't even knock on the bedroom door to check back with the tenant to tell them their outlets are working again. \n\nI just... move on. It drives my boss absolutely up the wall. He’s like, \"You did the hard work, you fixed the thing, why didn't you just push the button on the screen to say you fixed it?\" And I never have a good answer for him. It’s like once the puzzle is solved, my brain just wipes the rest of the steps completely clean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself losing or misplacing everyday items you need, like your keys, tools, phone, or paperwork, both at work and at home?\n\nPatient: Oh, it's a nightmare. It is an absolute nightmare. You asked about home, and sure, maybe I'll set my phone down on the kitchen counter and have to pat down my jacket pockets to find my car keys before I leave the house, but at work? At work, it is a constant, exhausting scavenger hunt. \n\nI am constantly—and I mean constantly—hunting for that new tablet they gave us, or my ring of master keys, or my flashlight. And it's not even just the big things, it’s the tool I literally just had in my hand a minute ago. I’ll be under a sink, right? I’ll have my favorite wrench—it has this bright yellow grip so I'm supposed to be able to see it easily in the dark—and I’ll use it, set it down on the mat for two seconds to grab a piece of Teflon tape, and when I reach back for the wrench, it’s gone. It’s like it just vanished into thin air. I’ll end up pulling everything out of my tool bag, checking all my pockets, crawling around on the floor... \n\nIt’s gotten bad enough that it actively holds up jobs. I'll be in a unit for twice as long as a basic repair should take just because I'm looking for my own equipment. And the tenants notice, you know? They’re standing there in their kitchens or living rooms, watching me spin in circles looking for a pair of wire strippers, and it just makes me look so incredibly careless. Like I’m some kind of amateur who doesn't respect her own tools. And I do know what I'm doing, I really do! I've been fixing things my whole life. But how can I prove to my supervisor that I'm ready for that senior position when I have to stop a job halfway through because I lost the flashlight I was just holding? It's just humiliating, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to stay focused during things like reading a book, sitting through a long conversation, or paying attention in meetings—even when you're trying your best to concentrate?\n\nPatient: Meetings? Well, we really only have our morning huddles for the maintenance crew, and I mostly just stand in the back and nod through those, but reading... yeah. Reading is a massive problem, especially with that new tablet system I was telling you about earlier. It’s supposed to make everything so streamlined, right? Management is always saying, \"Everything you need to know is in the notes!\" But it's... it's like my brain just refuses to hold onto the words, no matter how hard I try to lock in.\n\nI mean, here's exactly how it goes. I’ll get a ping for a new digital work order. I’ll pull it up, usually I'm standing right there in the hallway or down in the boiler room, and I’m looking at the screen thinking, okay, I’ve got it, let’s see what’s going on. And I start reading the notes the leasing office typed up—and sometimes they write a whole novel in there about what the tenant said, which really doesn't help, they put in all these details about the tenant's day instead of just telling me the sink is clogged—but anyway, I'm reading it. I am actively trying my absolute best to concentrate on the words. \n\nBut halfway through reading those notes, my mind just... it just slips right off. It completely drifts away. It doesn't even take much to derail me. I'll hear a noise down the hall, like an elevator door opening or a pipe clanking, or I'll just suddenly start thinking about the next job I have to do in unit 7A and whether I have the right drill bit for it. And my eyes are still moving down the screen, you know? Like I'm still physically looking at the tablet. But then I get to the bottom of the screen and I realize... I don't know what I just looked at. I haven't absorbed a single word of it. \n\nI literally have to go back to the top and read the exact same sentence three, four times just to figure out if I'm supposed to be fixing a garbage disposal or a thermostat. And if a tenant walks by and says hello while I'm trying to read it? Forget it. I have to start the whole process over from scratch. It’s exhausting, having to read the same three sentences over and over just to get my brain to actually register what they mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling persistently sad, anxious, or unusually stressed lately, or noticed changes in your sleep, appetite, or energy that started around the same time as these concentration problems?\n\nPatient: Well, sad? No, I wouldn't say sad. It passes as soon as I get out of there. \n\nAnd as for my sleep and appetite, those are perfectly fine. It's not like I woke up a few months ago and suddenly couldn't read a work order. It’s been this way... gosh, pretty much my whole life. I remember being exactly like this back in high school, just completely unable to hold onto a thought if I was reading a history textbook, or leaving my locker wide open in the hallway because I finished grabbing my jacket and my brain just completely moved on to the next thing. So, yeah, no new sadness or sleep issues or anything, and definitely no sudden changes, because the concentration stuff has just always, always been there.\n\nDoctor: Do you use alcohol, recreational drugs, or a lot of caffeine, or have you ever been told you have a thyroid problem or sleep condition like sleep apnea?\n\nPatient: Alcohol or drugs? Well, no, nobody's ever said anything about me snoring or stopping breathing or anything like that. I live alone, so I guess there's nobody right there to hear me anyway, but I've been on a few camping trips with my sister and her kids over the years—we usually go up to this state park about three hours north of here, beautiful place, but the bugs are terrible—and we've shared a cabin, and she's a really light sleeper, she wakes up if a pinecone hits the roof, and she's never once complained about me making noise in my sleep. And she would absolutely tell me if I did, believe me, she's not shy about that sort of thing. 

And as for feeling excessively sleepy during the day... well, like I said before, I get tired, sure. But it's physical tiredness from the job. It's not like I'm sitting in the truck at a red light and fighting to keep my eyes open, or hiding in a utility closet trying to catch a nap. I'm moving constantly from 7:30 AM until 4:00 PM. Sometimes I'm on my feet so much that my knees ache by the time I get home, especially if I've been kneeling on tile floors trying to snake a stubborn shower drain all afternoon. Those older buildings, the plumbing is just a nightmare, all galvanized pipes that are rusting from the inside out, so you really have to work at it. But even then, I'm not... I'm not sleepy, if that makes sense. I'm just physically worn out. My brain might be a little scrambled from trying to keep track of all the different work orders on that tablet, but I'm not nodding off or anything like that. I just need to sit in my recliner for a bit and rest my legs. So no, definitely no gasping for air at night or falling asleep during the day.

I’ve been doing it a long time, and I’m really good with my hands, but the administrative stuff... it’s just gotten completely out of hand. \n\nWhat really brought me in is that I just keep forgetting things. Important things. Like scheduled callbacks. A tenant will ask me to come back and check a valve once the pressure builds up, and I swear I mean to do it, but it just evaporates from my brain. And the work orders! They give us these tablets now, and I’ll look at a work order, put the tablet down to grab my wrench, and completely forget it exists. I’m missing basic follow-ups I promised people, too. Like, I'll tell someone I need to order a specific part and that I'll call them by Friday. Friday comes and goes, and I don't even remember the conversation until they complain to management. \n\nIt’s all just piled up so much lately. I have this huge backlog of half-finished tickets and angry voicemails. My boss finally pulled me into the office last week. There’s a lead maintenance position opening up, and he told me I’m the best fixer they have, but he basically said work sent me here to get my head straight before I lose my shot at moving up. If I don't get this under control, they're going to pass me over for the promotion, and I really, really need the pay bump, so... yeah. That's why I'm here. I just can't keep dropping the ball like this.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced this kind of forgetfulness and trouble following through on tasks for as long as you can remember, even back in school, or is this something that's really only become a problem recently?\n\nPatient: Oh, gosh, no, it’s not just recently. I mean, my boss probably thinks it’s just the new tablet system or the volume of tickets lately, but honestly? I remember being exactly this way way back in grade school. Like, long, long before I ever had to worry about job pressure or promotions or any of that adult stuff. \n\nI used to drive my teachers absolutely up the wall. They’d always tell my parents—I can still picture my fourth-grade teacher, Mrs. Higgins, at one of those parent-teacher conferences—she’d always say, \"She’s such a bright girl, she really is, but...\" There was always a \"but.\" And it was always about how I was constantly misplacing my papers. I’d do the homework, I swear I’d sit at the kitchen table and do it, and then by the time I got to school, the paper had just vanished into thin air. My backpack was like a black hole. \n\nOr they’d give us instructions for a project, and I’d somehow just completely miss parts of the directions. Like, I’d hear the first step, and maybe the last thing they said, but the middle part just didn't register at all. And doing actual work in class? If the teacher didn't come by and tap on my desk to give me a reminder, I’d never finish my classwork. I’d be off taking my pen apart to see how the spring worked or staring at the radiator. I always needed those constant reminders just to get through a basic worksheet. \n\nSo, yeah, it’s definitely not a new thing at all. It’s just... I guess when you’re a kid, adults are there to catch you when you drop the ball, you know? They tap your desk. But now it’s just me, and nobody's tapping my shoulder to remind me to call a tenant back about a leak, so it's just catching up to me so much worse now.\n\nDoctor: Growing up, and even now, have you often felt restless, fidgety, or like you had to be constantly moving, or found yourself acting impulsively—like blurting things out or making quick decisions without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: You know, it’s funny you ask that, because people always assume that since I work in maintenance and I’m on my feet all day, I must have a ton of energy. But honestly? No, not at all. I’ve never really been the restless type. \n\nI mean, my brother, now he was a holy terror growing up—always bouncing off the walls, couldn't sit still at the dinner table for two seconds without kicking his legs or tipping his chair back. But me? I was never like that. If I had to sit still, I could sit still. I'd just be completely zoned out while I did it. Even now, if I sit down in my recliner after a shift, I am perfectly content just sinking into the cushions. Half the time I sit there so long staring into space that I completely forget to make dinner until my stomach starts growling at nine o'clock at night. It's not a nervous energy where I feel like my motor is running, you know? \n\nAnd as for blurting things out or being impulsive... gosh, no. I wouldn't say that either. I'm actually pretty quiet. Sometimes I think I'm too quiet. Like, we have these Monday morning safety meetings at the shop, and the other guys are always cracking jokes, talking over each other, interrupting the supervisor. Not me. I’m usually just sitting there in the back row. I might be completely in my own world, you know, staring at a water stain on the ceiling tiles and trying to figure out if it's from a leaky roof or condensation from the HVAC duct, but I’m definitely not interrupting anybody. \n\nAnd making quick decisions? Oh, absolutely not. If anything, I take way, way too long to decide things. I will literally stand in the plumbing aisle at the supply house for twenty minutes just staring at two different brands of pipe thread sealant. I'll read the labels, put them back, pick them up again, trying to figure out which one is better, even though I know perfectly well they do the exact same thing and cost the exact same amount. My boss always tells me I overthink the small stuff. \n\nSo, yeah, no, I don't really get that restless, jumpy, impulsive feeling at all. I've always been more of a... well, just a slow-moving daydreamer, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you notice this same kind of forgetfulness and difficulty following through—like missing bill payments, losing track of household chores, or forgetting personal appointments—in your day-to-day home life as well?\n\nPatient: Well, you’d think so, right? If my brain is like a sieve at work, you’d think my house would be a complete disaster area. But honestly... no, I really haven't noticed it being a problem at home. \n\nTake the bills, for example. I don't think I've missed a payment in... gosh, maybe a decade? But that\n\nDoctor: When you say you haven't missed a bill payment in a decade, is that because you've set up things like autopay or reminders to handle it automatically, or do you actively remember and manage these tasks on your own without any special systems in place?\n\nPatient: Well, no, it’s not that I just naturally remember it all, I suppose. I mean, I do use autopay for the big stuff—the mortgage, the electric bill, the water bill. I set that up years ago because, well, why wouldn't you, right? It just makes sense. But even for the things that aren't on autopay, like my credit card or my internet bill, I just... I don't know, I just handle it. I usually sit down on Sunday mornings with my coffee—I have this specific mug, it’s got a chipped handle, but it’s my favorite—and I just go through the mail and pay whatever needs paying right then and there. It’s just a routine. I don't need alarms buzzing at me or anything like that.\n\nBut work? Work is a completely different story. That's... that's the whole reason I'm sitting in this chair right now, honestly. It’s the forgetfulness at work that’s completely out of control. I’ll get a call from a tenant in 4B about a leaky faucet, and I’ll say, \"Sure thing, Mrs. Gable, I’ll be up there in twenty minutes,\" and then I’ll hang up, and... poof. It’s gone. Completely gone from my brain. I’ll start walking toward the elevator, and then the property manager will stop me to ask about the snowblower maintenance, and by the time that conversation is over, I have absolutely no memory of Mrs. Gable or her faucet. \n\nAnd it’s not just one or two things, it’s everything. I’m forgetting scheduled callbacks, I’m losing track of work orders... I mean, we have this new tablet system where everything is supposed to be logged, but if I don't type it in the exact second I get the call, I forget to do it later. And I'm forgetting basic follow-ups I promised people. Like, I’ll tell a guy I’m going to check if we have a specific air filter in the supply closet, and three days later he’s asking me about it, and I’m just staring at him blankly because I never even went to the closet. \n\nIt’s just piled up so much. The complaints are stacking up. My supervisor finally pulled me into his office last week—he’s usually a pretty laid-back guy, you know, he used to be a plumber himself so he gets how chaotic the job can be—but he sat me down and said, \"Look, you do good work when you actually do it, but things are slipping through the cracks.\" There’s a senior maintenance position opening up next month, and I really, really want it. I need it, honestly. But he basically told me that work sent me here to get this figured out, because if I keep dropping the ball like this, I’m going to lose my shot at moving up. So it’s really just the work stuff that’s falling apart on me, not the home stuff.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work and paying bills, do you notice this same kind of forgetfulness or difficulty following through in other areas—like keeping up with hobbies, replying to texts or social plans, or finishing personal projects around the house?\n\nPatient: Honestly, with hobbies and texts... I don't really have much of a social life to mess up, to be perfectly honest with you. My sister texts me pictures of her dog, and I text back a thumbs up, and that's about it. And as for personal projects, by the time I get home from a shift, I'm usually just too beat to even start anything. \n\nBut when you say \"difficulty following through\"... I know you were asking about home stuff, but my mind just goes right back to the shop, because that is exactly the phrase my supervisor used last week. Follow-through. It’s like I just can't let it go, because it's driving me crazy. \n\nIt’s just... okay, here is exactly what happens. I’ll get a work order, right? Let's say it's an electrical issue in one of the older units on the third floor. I’ll haul all my gear up there, I’ll begin the maintenance job, I'll troubleshoot the whole thing—which, by the way, in those old buildings is a nightmare because the wiring was done in the seventies and half of it makes absolutely no sense—but I’ll figure it out. I’ll do the actual fix. I'll swap out the faulty breaker, strip the wires, test the circuit, make sure everything is perfectly safe and working better than before. The hard part is done! The problem is solved. \n\nBut then... I don't know what happens in my brain. It's like I mentally check out the exact second the physical problem is solved. I will somehow leave the last, easiest steps completely undone. I'll pack up my tool bag and just walk away without closing the ticket on the tablet, or I'll forget to use my marker and label the panel so the next guy knows what goes where, or I won't even knock on the bedroom door to check back with the tenant to tell them their outlets are working again. \n\nI just... move on. It drives my boss absolutely up the wall. It’s like once the puzzle is solved, my brain just wipes the rest of the steps completely clean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself losing or misplacing everyday items you need, like your keys, tools, phone, or paperwork, both at work and at home?\n\nPatient: Oh, it's a nightmare. It’s like it just vanished into thin air. I'll be in a unit for twice as long as a basic repair should take just because I'm looking for my own equipment. And the tenants notice, you know? It’s exhausting, having to read the same three sentences over and over just to get my brain to actually register what they mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling persistently sad, anxious, or unusually stressed lately, or noticed changes in your sleep, appetite, or energy that started around the same time as these concentration problems?\n\nPatient: Well, sad? I usually sit down on Sunday mornings with my coffee—I have this specific mug, it’s got a chipped handle, but it’s my favorite—and I just go through the mail and pay whatever needs paying right then and there. It’s just a routine. I don't need alarms buzzing at me or anything like that.\n\nBut work? Work is a completely different story. That's... that's the whole reason I'm sitting in this chair right now, honestly. It’s the forgetfulness at work that’s completely out of control. I’ll get a call from a tenant in 4B about a leaky faucet, and I’ll say, \"Sure thing, Mrs. Gable, I’ll be up there in twenty minutes,\" and then I’ll hang up, and... poof. It’s gone. Completely gone from my brain. I’ll start walking toward the elevator, and then the property manager will stop me to ask about the snowblower maintenance, and by the time that conversation is over, I have absolutely no memory of Mrs. Gable or her faucet. \n\nAnd it’s not just one or two things, it’s everything. I’m forgetting scheduled callbacks, I’m losing track of work orders... I mean, we have this new tablet system where everything is supposed to be logged, but if I don't type it in the exact second I get the call, I forget to do it later. And I'm forgetting basic follow-ups I promised people. Like, I’ll tell a guy I’m going to check if we have a specific air filter in the supply closet, and three days later he’s asking me about it, and I’m just staring at him blankly because I never even went to the closet. \n\nIt’s just piled up so much. The complaints are stacking up. My supervisor finally pulled me into his office last week—he’s usually a pretty laid-back guy, you know, he used to be a plumber himself so he gets how chaotic the job can be—but he sat me down and said, \"Look, you do good work when you actually do it, but things are slipping through the cracks.\" There’s a senior maintenance position opening up next month, and I really, really want it. I need it, honestly. But he basically told me that work sent me here to get this figured out, because if I keep dropping the ball like this, I’m going to lose my shot at moving up. So it’s really just the work stuff that’s falling apart on me, not the home stuff.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work and paying bills, do you notice this same kind of forgetfulness or difficulty following through in other areas—like keeping up with hobbies, replying to texts or social plans, or finishing personal projects around the house?\n\nPatient: Honestly, with hobbies and texts... I don't really have much of a social life to mess up, to be perfectly honest with you. My sister texts me pictures of her dog, and I text back a thumbs up, and that's about it. And as for personal projects, by the time I get home from a shift, I'm usually just too beat to even start anything. \n\nBut when you say \"difficulty following through\"... I know you were asking about home stuff, but my mind just goes right back to the shop, because that is exactly the phrase my supervisor used last week. Follow-through. It’s like I just can't let it go, because it's driving me crazy. \n\nIt’s just... okay, here is exactly what happens. I’ll get a work order, right? Let's say it's an electrical issue in one of the older units on the third floor. I’ll haul all my gear up there, I’ll begin the maintenance job, I'll troubleshoot the whole thing—which, by the way, in those old buildings is a nightmare because the wiring was done in the seventies and half of it makes absolutely no sense—but I’ll figure it out. I’ll do the actual fix. I'll swap out the faulty breaker, strip the wires, test the circuit, make sure everything is perfectly safe and working better than before. The hard part is done! The problem is solved. \n\nBut then... I don't know what happens in my brain. It's like I mentally check out the exact second the physical problem is solved. I will somehow leave the last, easiest steps completely undone. I'll pack up my tool bag and just walk away without closing the ticket on the tablet, or I'll forget to use my marker and label the panel so the next guy knows what goes where, or I won't even knock on the bedroom door to check back with the tenant to tell them their outlets are working again. \n\nI just... move on. It drives my boss absolutely up the wall. He’s like, \"You did the hard work, you fixed the thing, why didn't you just push the button on the screen to say you fixed it?\" And I never have a good answer for him. It’s like once the puzzle is solved, my brain just wipes the rest of the steps completely clean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself losing or misplacing everyday items you need, like your keys, tools, phone, or paperwork, both at work and at home?\n\nPatient: Oh, it's a nightmare. It is an absolute nightmare. You asked about home, and sure, maybe I'll set my phone down on the kitchen counter and have to pat down my jacket pockets to find my car keys before I leave the house, but at work? At work, it is a constant, exhausting scavenger hunt. \n\nI am constantly—and I mean constantly—hunting for that new tablet they gave us, or my ring of master keys, or my flashlight. And it's not even just the big things, it’s the tool I literally just had in my hand a minute ago. I’ll be under a sink, right? I’ll have my favorite wrench—it has this bright yellow grip so I'm supposed to be able to see it easily in the dark—and I’ll use it, set it down on the mat for two seconds to grab a piece of Teflon tape, and when I reach back for the wrench, it’s gone. It’s like it just vanished into thin air. I’ll end up pulling everything out of my tool bag, checking all my pockets, crawling around on the floor... \n\nIt’s gotten bad enough that it actively holds up jobs. I'll be in a unit for twice as long as a basic repair should take just because I'm looking for my own equipment. And the tenants notice, you know? They’re standing there in their kitchens or living rooms, watching me spin in circles looking for a pair of wire strippers, and it just makes me look so incredibly careless. Like I’m some kind of amateur who doesn't respect her own tools. And I do know what I'm doing, I really do! I've been fixing things my whole life. But how can I prove to my supervisor that I'm ready for that senior position when I have to stop a job halfway through because I lost the flashlight I was just holding? It's just humiliating, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to stay focused during things like reading a book, sitting through a long conversation, or paying attention in meetings—even when you're trying your best to concentrate?\n\nPatient: Meetings? Well, we really only have our morning huddles for the maintenance crew, and I mostly just stand in the back and nod through those, but reading... yeah. Reading is a massive problem, especially with that new tablet system I was telling you about earlier. It’s supposed to make everything so streamlined, right? Management is always saying, \"Everything you need to know is in the notes!\" But it's... it's like my brain just refuses to hold onto the words, no matter how hard I try to lock in.\n\nI mean, here's exactly how it goes. I’ll get a ping for a new digital work order. I’ll pull it up, usually I'm standing right there in the hallway or down in the boiler room, and I’m looking at the screen thinking, okay, I’ve got it, let’s see what’s going on. And I start reading the notes the leasing office typed up—and sometimes they write a whole novel in there about what the tenant said, which really doesn't help, they put in all these details about the tenant's day instead of just telling me the sink is clogged—but anyway, I'm reading it. I am actively trying my absolute best to concentrate on the words. \n\nBut halfway through reading those notes, my mind just... it just slips right off. It completely drifts away. It doesn't even take much to derail me. I'll hear a noise down the hall, like an elevator door opening or a pipe clanking, or I'll just suddenly start thinking about the next job I have to do in unit 7A and whether I have the right drill bit for it. And my eyes are still moving down the screen, you know? Like I'm still physically looking at the tablet. But then I get to the bottom of the screen and I realize... I don't know what I just looked at. I haven't absorbed a single word of it. \n\nI literally have to go back to the top and read the exact same sentence three, four times just to figure out if I'm supposed to be fixing a garbage disposal or a thermostat. And if a tenant walks by and says hello while I'm trying to read it? Forget it. I have to start the whole process over from scratch. It’s exhausting, having to read the same three sentences over and over just to get my brain to actually register what they mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling persistently sad, anxious, or unusually stressed lately, or noticed changes in your sleep, appetite, or energy that started around the same time as these concentration problems?\n\nPatient: Well, sad? No, I wouldn't say sad. I mean, I get frustrated, obviously—like I was just telling you with the whole flashlight situation and my supervisor getting on my case—but I'm not, you know, crying in my truck or feeling down about life in general. I actually really like my job! I like working with my hands and figuring out how things tick. It's just the digital tablets and the details that drive me nuts. So, no, not persistently sad or depressed or anything like that. And stressed... well, sure, I get stressed when a tenant is standing there with their arms crossed while I'm tearing apart my tool bag looking for a simple wire nut, but it's just in the moment, you know? It passes as soon as I get out of there. \n\nAnd as for my sleep and appetite, those are perfectly fine. I mean, I'm pretty beat at the end of the day, but I'm hauling heavy gear up and down three flights of stairs in older buildings where the elevators are down half the week, so of course I'm tired! But I sleep like a log the second my head hits the pillow. And my appetite hasn't changed a bit. I pack the exact same ham and swiss sandwich and a big thermos of black coffee every single morning, eat it in the truck around noon, never miss a meal. \n\nBut the main thing you just said—you asked if any of that started around the same time as these concentration problems. And... well, that's the thing. There isn't really a \"same time.\" Because these problems with focusing and losing my tools and checking out early? They didn't just start lately. It's not like I woke up a few months ago and suddenly couldn't read a work order. It’s been this way... gosh, pretty much my whole life. I remember being exactly like this back in high school, just completely unable to hold onto a thought if I was reading a history textbook, or leaving my locker wide open in the hallway because I finished grabbing my jacket and my brain just completely moved on to the next thing. So, yeah, no new sadness or sleep issues or anything, and definitely no sudden changes, because the concentration stuff has just always, always been there.\n\nDoctor: Do you use alcohol, recreational drugs, or a lot of caffeine, or have you ever been told you have a thyroid problem or sleep condition like sleep apnea?\n\nPatient: Alcohol or drugs? Oh, definitely not. I mean, I might have a single beer if I'm at my brother's house for a summer barbecue or something, but that is absolutely it. Recreational drugs, never. In my line of work, you really can't be messing around with anything like that anyway. I mean, I'm dealing with heavy power tools, high-voltage electrical wiring, main plumbing lines where if you mess up, you could flood an entire apartment building. You make one careless mistake with a 220-volt line because your head isn't clear, and that's a really bad day, you know? Plus, the management company does random screenings for the maintenance staff, and I have been at this job way too long to risk my livelihood over something foolish. \n\nNow, caffeine... you asked about a lot of caffeine. I guess it\n\nDoctor: Has anyone ever told you that you snore loudly, gasp, or stop breathing for moments while you're asleep, or do you often feel excessively sleepy during the day even after a full night's sleep?\n\nPatient: Well, no, nobody's ever said anything about me snoring or stopping breathing or anything like that. I live alone, so I guess there's nobody right there to hear me anyway, but I've been on a few camping trips with my sister and her kids over the years—we usually go up to this state park about three hours north of here, beautiful place, but the bugs are terrible—and we've shared a cabin, and she's a really light sleeper, she wakes up if a pinecone hits the roof, and she's never once complained about me making noise in my sleep. And she would absolutely tell me if I did, believe me, she's not shy about that sort of thing. \n\nAnd as for feeling excessively sleepy during the day... well, like I said before, I get tired, sure. But it's physical tiredness from the job. It's not like I'm sitting in the truck at a red light and fighting to keep my eyes open, or hiding in a utility closet trying to catch a nap. I'm moving constantly from 7:30 AM until 4:00 PM. Sometimes I'm on my feet so much that my knees ache by the time I get home, especially if I've been kneeling on tile floors trying to snake a stubborn shower drain all afternoon. Those older buildings, the plumbing is just a nightmare, all galvanized pipes that are rusting from the inside out, so you really have to work at it. But even then, I'm not... I'm not sleepy, if that makes sense. I'm just physically worn out. My brain might be a little scrambled from trying to keep track of all the different work orders on that tablet, but I'm not nodding off or anything like that. I just need to sit in my recliner for a bit and rest my legs. So no, definitely no gasping for air at night or falling asleep during the day."

Subtypes: Auditory Hallucinations, Visual Hallucinations, Tactile Hallucinations, Olfactory Hallucinations, Gustatory Hallucinations\n[S021] Disorganized_Thinking_Speech: Symptoms where speech or thought appears scattered and difficult to follow due to a lack of logical or consistent flow.  Subtypes: Derailment or Loose Associations, Tangentiality, Incoherence or Word Salad, Neologisms, Clang Associations\n[S022] Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior: A state where behavior and motor control are severely disturbed, appearing as movements or reactions inappropriate to the situation.  Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event. Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nYou know, it’s funny you ask that, because people always assume that since I work in maintenance and I’m on my feet all day, I must have a ton of energy. But honestly? No, not at all. I’ve never really been the restless type. \n\nI mean, my brother, now he was a holy terror growing up—always bouncing off the walls, couldn't sit still at the dinner table for two seconds without kicking his legs or tipping his chair back. But me? I was never like that. If I had to sit still, I could sit still. I'd just be completely zoned out while I did it. Even now, if I sit down in my recliner after a shift, I am perfectly content just sinking into the cushions. Half the time I sit there so long staring into space that I completely forget to make dinner until my stomach starts growling at nine o'clock at night. It's not a nervous energy where I feel like my motor is running, you know? \n\nAnd as for blurting things out or being impulsive... gosh, no. I wouldn't say that either. I'm actually pretty quiet. Sometimes I think I'm too quiet. Like, we have these Monday morning safety meetings at the shop, and the other guys are always cracking jokes, talking over each other, interrupting the supervisor. Not me. I’m usually just sitting there in the back row. I might be completely in my own world, you know, staring at a water stain on the ceiling tiles and trying to figure out if it's from a leaky roof or condensation from the HVAC duct, but I’m definitely not interrupting anybody. \n\nAnd making quick decisions? Oh, absolutely not. If anything, I take way, way too long to decide things. I will literally stand in the plumbing aisle at the supply house for twenty minutes just staring at two different brands of pipe thread sealant. I'll read the labels, put them back, pick them up again, trying to figure out which one is better, even though I know perfectly well they do the exact same thing and cost the exact same amount. My boss always tells me I overthink the small stuff. \n\nSo, yeah, no, I don't really get that restless, jumpy, impulsive feeling at all. I've always been more of a... well, just a slow-moving daydreamer, I guess.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep. Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions). Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol). Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nWell, no, it’s not that I just naturally remember it all, I suppose. I mean, I do use autopay for the big stuff—the mortgage, the electric bill, the water bill. I set that up years ago because, well, why wouldn't you, right? It just makes sense. But even for the things that aren't on autopay, like my credit card or my internet bill, I just... I don't know, I just handle it. I usually sit down on Sunday mornings with my coffee—I have this specific mug, it’s got a chipped handle, but it’s my favorite—and I just go through the mail and pay whatever needs paying right then and there. It’s just a routine. I don't need alarms buzzing at me or anything like that.\n\nBut work? Work is a completely different story. That's... that's the whole reason I'm sitting in this chair right now, honestly. It’s the forgetfulness at work that’s completely out of control. I’ll get a call from a tenant in 4B about a leaky faucet, and I’ll say, \"Sure thing, Mrs. Gable, I’ll be up there in twenty minutes,\" and then I’ll hang up, and... poof. It’s gone. Completely gone from my brain. I’ll start walking toward the elevator, and then the property manager will stop me to ask about the snowblower maintenance, and by the time that conversation is over, I have absolutely no memory of Mrs. Gable or her faucet. \n\nAnd it’s not just one or two things, it’s everything. I’m forgetting scheduled callbacks, I’m losing track of work orders... I mean, we have this new tablet system where everything is supposed to be logged, but if I don't type it in the exact second I get the call, I forget to do it later. And I'm forgetting basic follow-ups I promised people. Like, I’ll tell a guy I’m going to check if we have a specific air filter in the supply closet, and three days later he’s asking me about it, and I’m just staring at him blankly because I never even went to the closet. \n\nIt’s just piled up so much. The complaints are stacking up. My supervisor finally pulled me into his office last week—he’s usually a pretty laid-back guy, you know, he used to be a plumber himself so he gets how chaotic the job can be—but he sat me down and said, \"Look, you do good work when you actually do it, but things are slipping through the cracks.\" There’s a senior maintenance position opening up next month, and I really, really want it. I need it, honestly. But he basically told me that work sent me here to get this figured out, because if I keep dropping the ball like this, I’m going to lose my shot at moving up. So it’s really just the work stuff that’s falling apart on me, not the home stuff.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. I know you were asking about home stuff, but my mind just goes right back to the shop, because that is exactly the phrase my supervisor used last week. Follow-through. It’s like I just can't let it go, because it's driving me crazy. \n\nIt’s just... okay, here is exactly what happens. I’ll get a work order, right? Let's say it's an electrical issue in one of the older units on the third floor. I’ll haul all my gear up there, I’ll begin the maintenance job, I'll troubleshoot the whole thing—which, by the way, in those old buildings is a nightmare because the wiring was done in the seventies and half of it makes absolutely no sense—but I’ll figure it out. I’ll do the actual fix. I'll swap out the faulty breaker, strip the wires, test the circuit, make sure everything is perfectly safe and working better than before. The hard part is done! The problem is solved. \n\nBut then... I don't know what happens in my brain. It's like I mentally check out the exact second the physical problem is solved. I will somehow leave the last, easiest steps completely undone. I'll pack up my tool bag and just walk away without closing the ticket on the tablet, or I'll forget to use my marker and label the panel so the next guy knows what goes where, or I won't even knock on the bedroom door to check back with the tenant to tell them their outlets are working again. \n\nI just... move on. It drives my boss absolutely up the wall. He’s like, \"You did the hard work, you fixed the thing, why didn't you just push the button on the screen to say you fixed it?\" And I never have a good answer for him. Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.
